### 2.4 Programme Monitoring

#### 5.7 Monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) arrangements (see 300 words, approximately 1 page)

##### Programme Benchmarks

- It will be done as Accredited Entity. SPC will conduct and supervise the implementation of the project. It accommodates the needs of the target communities.
- The monitoring system will be designed to ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the approved project document, including the logical framework (Logframe), and the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP). These documents are subject to periodic review by SPC.
- The monitoring system will include a baseline survey, regular progress reports, and an end-of-project evaluation. The baseline survey will be conducted at the beginning of the project, and the progress reports will be submitted annually.
- The monitoring system will support the implementation of the project and ensure that it is on track to achieve its objectives. It will also provide information on the impact of the project on the target communities.

##### 10. Support M&E staff

- SPC will support M&E staff, assist in programme. This will include establishing M&E systems that are aligned with GEF, Non-Governmental Organizations (NGO) and other donor requirements.
- The M&E system will be designed to ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the approved project document, including the logical framework (Logframe), and the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP). These documents are subject to periodic review by SPC.
- The monitoring system will include a baseline survey, regular progress reports, and an end-of-project evaluation. The baseline survey will be conducted at the beginning of the project, and the progress reports will be submitted annually.

##### 10. Review and update M&E tools

- SPC will review and update M&E tools that have been used in previous projects. This will ensure that the tools are up-to-date and effective.
- The monitoring system will support the implementation of the project and ensure that it is on track to achieve its objectives. It will also provide information on the impact of the project on the target communities.

##### 10. For the indicator outputs, specific through the EDF Facility, region M&E staff at the sub-national level of SPC will be responsible for monitoring and evaluation.

- The monitoring system will support the implementation of the project and ensure that it is on track to achieve its objectives. It will also provide information on the impact of the project on the target communities.
- The monitoring system will include a baseline survey, regular progress reports, and an end-of-project evaluation. The baseline survey will be conducted at the beginning of the project, and the progress reports will be submitted annually.

##### 10. For Category B activities, a sub-national level should be designed jointly and verified by SPC to ensure that the monitoring system is effective.

- The monitoring system will support the implementation of the project and ensure that it is on track to achieve its objectives. It will also provide information on the impact of the project on the target communities.
- The monitoring system will include a baseline survey, regular progress reports, and an end-of-project evaluation. The baseline survey will be conducted at the beginning of the project, and the progress reports will be submitted annually.

### Programme Evaluation

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 18 OF 19

## 10.0 The following evaluation exercises will be carried out as part of the M&E Framework only if proposed by ECU and SPC CIF:

- Continuous evaluation of the sub-projects as they are completed (to be carried out by ECU and SPC CIF).
  - A National M&E expert will conduct evaluations of sub-projects with a view to update the catalogue of lessons learned.
  - The M&E expert will be guided by a national ESS and QESI expert report on environmental, social and economic impacts.
- An external pool of thematic experts will carry out expert analyses and assessments as relevant to the sub-projects.
  - This will ensure the integrity and efficacy of evaluation recommendations, as per clause 2.1.3.
- An independent consultant will be engaged to conduct an evaluation of the overall project implementation.
  - The evaluation report will be carried out by an independent consultant and to be completed within 6 months after the end of project implementation.

## 10.1 The ECU and SPC Team will be given responsibility for overseeing the aforementioned exercise and they will also be responsible for ensuring that:
- The objectives of the sub-projects are achieved.
- The objectives of the sub-projects are achieved in accordance with the approved Terms of Reference (ToR).
  - This will include the following: environmental, social and economic impacts; gender equality; climate change mitigation and adaptation; and working towards the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) as per the approved ToR.
- The evaluation should assess the degree to which the sub-projects have contributed to the objectives of the project.
  - This will include changes in FSM, both qualitative and quantitative, and contribute to the evidence base for replication to bring about change in FSM practices.

## F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

### 1. Risk factors and strategies assessed (max. 3 pages)

| Category                | Probability | Impact |
|-------------------------|-------------|--------|
| **Governance**          | Low         | High   |
| Description             | Lack of oversight from LAs leads to underfunded project failure and missed report for the LGA Family and Friends Programme. |
| **Diagrams and Maps**    | Medium      | High   |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v.2 | PAGE 19 OF 19

## (Mission Statement)

The core purpose of the EDA Programme is to develop and implement projects that build local and regional resilience to climate change, support green economic recovery, and promote sustainable development. The programme will be delivered through a combination of direct investment in projects, capacity building, and knowledge sharing. The programme will also provide technical assistance to help deliver these objectives.

#### Location (read and information on) infrastructure to help support development of energy projects, leading to climate resilience. This will include the provision of technical assistance and capacity building.

The EDA Programme works closely with national and state agencies to utilise existing climate vulnerability, and risk assessments to identify priority areas for investment. The programme also seeks to address the difficulties in sourcing technology and other inputs for Programme activities.

# G. GOALS AND STANDARDS

## 1. Environmental and Social Risk Management Issues

Environmental and social risk management issues have been identified in a general review of the environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) process for projects funded by the Green Climate Fund (GCF). These issues include:

- **Programmes being classified as Category I** according to GCF’s Environmental and Social Policy.
- **Ongoing or legacy issues due to student scholarships**.
- **Ongoing or legacy issues due to small grants and microfinance projects**.
- **Note on grievances**: Grievances are not addressed in the ESIA process.

### 1.1 Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA)

Environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) is a process that identifies the environmental and social risks and impacts of proposed projects, programmes, or policies throughout their lifecycle. The ESIA process includes:

- **Identification** of potential adverse environmental and social impacts.
- **Assessment** of significance and likelihood of these impacts.
- **Mitigation** measures to avoid or minimize adverse impacts.

### 1.2 Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS)

An ESMS is a management system that ensures the effective implementation of mitigation measures identified in an ESIA. The ESMS includes:

- **Environmental and social management plans**.
- **Monitoring and reporting systems**.

### 1.3 Stakeholder Engagement and Heritage

Stakeholder engagement is essential to ensure that projects are implemented with the participation of affected people, including indigenous peoples and local communities (IPLCs). This ensures that their rights, needs, and priorities are taken into account.

### 1.4 Grievance Redress Mechanisms

Grievance redress mechanisms enable individuals or groups who claim to have been adversely affected by a project to seek resolution of their grievances. These mechanisms should be:

- **Independent** from the implementing entity.
- **Accessible** to all affected people, including IPLCs.

### 1.5 Environmental and Social Safeguards

Environmental and social safeguards are measures designed to avoid or minimize adverse environmental and social impacts of projects. These include:

- **Safeguard standards** that have been adopted by the World Bank Group (WBG) and other multilateral development banks.
- **Safeguard policies** that have been adopted by the WBG.

### 1.6 Safeguards in GCF-Funded Projects

GCF-funded projects must adhere to the safeguard standards of the entity providing the funds, including those of the WBG and other multilateral development banks (MDBs). These safeguards apply to all projects funded by the GCF.

### 1.7 Safeguards in Non-Grant Projects

Non-grant projects are not subject to the same level of environmental and social risk management as grant projects. However, they must still adhere to any applicable laws and regulations.

## 2. General Risk Management Principles

General risk management principles apply to all GCF-funded projects, programmes, or policies. These include:

- **Identification** of risks.
- **Assessment** of the likelihood and impact of these risks.
- **Mitigation** measures to avoid or minimize adverse impacts.

### 2.1 Grievance Redress Mechanisms

Grievance redress mechanisms enable individuals or groups who claim to have been adversely affected by a project to seek resolution of their grievances. These mechanisms should be:

- **Independent** from the implementing entity.
- **Accessible** to all affected people, including IPLCs.

### 2.2 Environmental and Social Safeguards

Environmental and social safeguards are measures designed to avoid or minimize adverse environmental and social impacts of projects. These include:

- **Safeguard standards** that have been adopted by the WBG and other MDBs.
- **Safeguard policies** that have been adopted by the WBG.

### 2.3 Safeguards in GCF-Funded Projects

GCF-funded projects must adhere to the safeguard standards of the entity providing the funds, including those of the WBG and other MDBs. These safeguards apply to all projects funded by the GCF.

## 17
An initial developer workshop took place in October 2019. The workshop was conducted between SPC and its accredited entities, the Pacific Community (SPC), the Pacific Islands Development Assistance (PIDA) office, the Fiji Department of Resources and Development, GCF focal points from the region, and other relevant organizations such as the Secretariat of the Pacific Regional Environment Programme (SPREP). The workshop was held to discuss the development of a regional accreditation strategy for the Pacific. It also aimed at sharing experiences and lessons learned by other regional organizations (e.g., Global Green Growth Institute (GGGI), Green Climate Fund (GCF) National Designated Authorities (NDAs)).

## 18
The workshop was attended by representatives from the following countries: Fiji, Papua New Guinea, Samoa, Solomon Islands, Tonga, Vanuatu. The workshop was held in Suva, Fiji on November 20-21, 2019. Due to logistical issues, representatives from Kiribati and Tuvalu were not able to attend the workshop. The workshop was attended by representatives from the following organizations: the Pacific Islands Development Assistance (PIDA) office, the Fiji Department of Resources and Development, the Secretariat of the Pacific Regional Environment Programme (SPREP), the Global Green Growth Institute (GGGI), the Green Climate Fund (GCF) National Designated Authorities (NDAs).

Interested and affected are informed of all expenditures made under the Programme’s budget. All entities involved in the Programme’s implementation will be required to submit a financial report on their expenditure. This will ensure that the funds are used appropriately, in accordance with the GCF Financial Management Information System (FMIS) guidelines.

## 18
The FMIS is a Microsoft Dynamics NAV-based enterprise resource planning system designed to support the management of financial and non-financial resources. Using FMIS enables the flow of financial information, improves business processes, and enhances the efficiency of operations. It also ensures compliance with regulations of the United Nations rules and procedures for donor and project management.

## 19
During implementation, SPC will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies and procedures. This includes ensuring that all entities involved in the Programme are subject to SPC’s audit requirements. The audit will include the annual audit and financial statements of the Programme entities.

## 20
SPC’s Chief Financial Officer (CFO) will be responsible for providing a programme management and financial oversight, comprising a review of the Programme’s financial reports and ensuring that all funds are used in accordance with the approved budget. The CFO will also supervise the work of the ECF Internal Audit Unit (IAU), which includes a Programme Audit Unit.

## 21
The financial and operational requirements of the sub-programme proposed through the GCF will be undertaken in line with the provisions of the Operational Manual (see Annex 2). Disbursements of funds to sub-programmes will be made in accordance with the approved budget. The CFO will ensure that all financial reports are submitted to the Programme Board and the Executive Committee on a quarterly basis.

## 22
Disbursement of funds from the GCF to SPC will be subject to the approval of the ECF Internal Audit Unit (IAU). The IAU will review the financial statements of the sub-programme entities and ensure that they comply with the requirements of the Programme. The IAU will also provide oversight on the use of funds by the sub-programmes.

## 23
Disbursement of funds from SPC to the sub-programmes will be subject to the approval of the ECF Internal Audit Unit (IAU). The IAU will review the financial statements of the sub-programme entities and ensure that they comply with the requirements of the Programme. The IAU will also provide oversight on the use of funds by the sub-programmes.

The funding proposal is presented in the format required by consideration with the following assessment.

| Strengths | Points of concern |
| --- | --- |
| The proposed undertaking draws on existing (EDF) pilot projects and a regional approach to address climate change adaptation needs. | The project proposals yet to be developed need to be clearly defined and linked to the objectives of the SPC CEAS programme. The project proposals should be aligned with the IATAs, and the risks that cannot be assumed by the EDF funding opportunity should be clearly identified. |
| Fully compliant with the EDF requirements regarding: | Component 1 is crucial to increase the recovery capacity of the local authorities and communities in the Pacific. However, the project needs to ensure that the proposed activities are aligned with the objectives of the programme of cooperation, and this could be further clarified. |
| - Preparation and development of decision-making tools for climate change adaptation based on the community level and the national level; | The project is a Regional Technical Assistance (RTA) component and the project needs to ensure that the proposed activities are aligned with the objectives of the programme of cooperation, and this could be further clarified. |
| - Strengthening capacity and long-term sustainability of local authorities in the Pacific through the RTA as an operational facility | The Board may wish to consider operating this funding proposal with the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and additional SPC funds. Last year’s proposal conditions and objectives should be aligned with the current proposal. |

# 2.1. Programme background

The Federal State of Mecklenburg-Vorpommern (FSM) is a highly developed archipelago located in the North-East Sea. FSM formed the basis (Ostseeküste, Darß, Pütnitz and Thi) and has been part of the German Democratic Republic since 1945. The FSM consists of the mainland and the islands Rügen and Usedom. # Green Climate Fund

## E. Approximate 40 per cent of households in FSM live within 180 metres of the shoreline with a lack of terrain having the greatest impact on coastal flooding hazards (19% per cent of households) and storm surge hazards (35% per cent). At least 13 islands of the country trip and 12 per cent live within 1 kilometre. Additionally, 80 per cent of the population lives in areas that are less than 5 metres above sea level, with 60 per cent living in areas that are less than 1 metre above sea level. For the majority of operational conditions between RMI and their respective regions, the FSM is considered to be highly vulnerable to climate change impacts.

This is particularly true when considering the impact of sea-level rise on the country's freshwater resources. The FSM has a high dependence on groundwater for drinking water supplies, which are particularly susceptible to saltwater intrusion in low-lying atolls. The vulnerability of the FSM to climate change is exacerbated by its small size and limited capacity to adapt.

## 2.2 Components-by-component analysis

### Component I: intended to address barriers to protected climate impacts by the local communities and municipalities, as well as support and prepare the project proponents for the benefits of climate resilience.

The accredited entity (AE) will develop an adaptation measure inventory framework, which will include a risk assessment methodology that is tailored to the specific needs of the country. The AE will also provide technical assistance to the local governments in order to improve their capacity to implement adaptation measures.

### Component II: Long-term and short-term activities intended to address climate change adaptation actions that help build resilience to climate impacts.

Under this component, the accredited entity (AE) will develop an adaptation measure inventory framework, which will include a risk assessment methodology that is tailored to the specific needs of the country. The AE will also provide technical assistance to the local governments in order to improve their capacity to implement adaptation measures.

enhancing green bonds (GBs) facility to facilitate bond government officials with the aspects of the activity, best practices, eligibility criteria, and details of the three thematic areas that GBs can support: climate change mitigation, adaptation, and natural infrastructure. The facility will also provide technical assistance to governments in designing and implementing projects, including developing adaptation strategies for the country and trans-boundary development on shared waters.

Government officials will also be supported under this component, and will include learning exchanges with other countries that have successfully issued GBs. This component will include an exchange program for government officials and a data repository of disaggregated data at the national and sub-national levels on the three thematic areas.

# Green Investment Bank

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact potential

To ensure that the investment case remains valid, the project has significant potential at reduced scale. The project is designed to be modular and can be scaled up or down depending on market demand. Other projects in the pipeline will also benefit from this approach.

The RGF will undertake activities to substantiate that address at the local level that and current protection activities. These activities are at addressing the impact of a project on biodiversity, including the conservation of species and habitats. The RGF will be supported by the DfID project team to ensure that these activities are undertaken in accordance with best practice. The RGF will also support the development of a well-being assessment tool to monitor the environmental and social impacts of the project.

One of the existing projects of this proposal is to engage on emerging technical assistance opportunities to support the RGF's objectives. This includes (i) developing capacity building activities, including training and mentoring, to support the implementation of the RGF's objectives; (ii) supporting the development of a well-being assessment tool to monitor the environmental and social impacts of the project.

# Sustainable development potential

The proposed EDA can contribute to the achievement of several sustainable development goals (SDGs). It will support the conservation and restoration of marine ecosystems and biodiversity, which is a key component of SDG 14 (Life Below Water). The project will also enhance coastal resilience by reducing erosion and improving water quality, contributing to SDG 11 (Sustainable Cities and Communities) and SDG 6 (Clean Water and Sanitation).

In addition, the EDA can support improved agricultural productivity and food security through enhanced soil health and nutrient cycling, aligning with SDG 2 (Zero Hunger). The project will also contribute to SDG 8 (Decent Work and Economic Growth) by creating jobs in the blue economy sector. Furthermore, the EDA can help reduce greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture, supporting SDG 13 (Climate Action).

## Needs of the recipient

YSB is located in a region that relies heavily on small-scale fishing and aquaculture. YSB's fleet consists of over 400 boats, with populations above the legally prescribed safety limit. It is noted that the EDA pilot project could help address these issues by providing modern equipment and training to the recipients.

The larger activities that the RCOF can finance are not revenue generating and the focus will be on capacity building and technical assistance. The RCOF will support YSB in developing a business plan for the long-term sustainability of the EDA pilot project, including the identification of potential funding sources. This will help to ensure the long-term viability of the project.

Economic benefits are expected from enhanced agricultural productivity and improved water quality, which can lead to increased income and job creation. The project will also contribute to the development of the blue economy that could result in new industries and local services.

The intervention has strong potential on gender equality and will increase the participation of women in the blue economy sector. This is an important aspect of SDG 5 (Gender Equality).

## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

#### Programme background
The GIA will be designed to comprehensively capture the effectiveness of interventions that are vulnerable to the impacts of extreme climate events (e.g., flooding, droughts). It will encompass the following components: (i) a baseline assessment of environmental and social risks; (ii) an evaluation of the project's exposure to these risks; and (iii) recommendations for risk management strategies. The GIA will also include an analysis of the project's potential impacts on biodiversity and ecosystem services, as well as an assessment of the project's compliance with relevant safeguard policies.

#### Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instruments
The project is categorised under the environmental and social risk category (ESRC) 1. The GIA will focus on assessing the project's exposure to environmental and social risks and its potential impacts. The safeguards applied include: (i) a baseline assessment of environmental and social risks; (ii) an evaluation of the project's exposure to these risks; and (iii) recommendations for risk management strategies.

#### Safeguard requirements

# Compliance with G7 environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards

The document below describe how the project complies with G7 ESS standards.

## ESS 1: Labour and working conditions

The programme is expected to have positive impacts on labour and working conditions, including:

- **Economic growth**: The programme will generate employment opportunities for local communities.
- **Skills development**: The programme will provide training and skills development opportunities for local communities.
- **Decent work**: The programme will promote decent work practices, including fair wages, safe working conditions, and equal opportunities for all.

## ESS 2: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The programme is expected to have positive impacts on resource efficiency and pollution prevention, including:

- **Water management**: The programme will implement water-saving technologies and practices.
- **Waste management**: The programme will promote waste reduction and recycling.
- **Energy efficiency**: The programme will adopt energy-efficient technologies and practices.

## ESS 3: Community health, safety and security

The programme is expected to have positive impacts on community health, safety and security, including:

- **Health services**: The programme will provide access to healthcare services for local communities.
- **Safety measures**: The programme will implement safety measures to prevent accidents and injuries.
- **Security**: The programme will ensure the security of project assets and personnel.

## ESS 4: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The programme is expected to have positive impacts on land acquisition and involuntary resettlement, including:

- **Fair compensation**: The programme will provide fair compensation for land acquired.
- **Resettlement planning**: The programme will develop and implement resettlement plans.

## ESS 5: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The programme is expected to have positive impacts on biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources, including:

- **Habitat protection**: The programme will protect habitats for endangered species.
- **Sustainable use**: The programme will promote the sustainable use of natural resources.

## Introduction

The AI Act aims to establish a robust legal framework for artificial intelligence (AI), ensuring that it is developed and used in compliance with EU values and laws. The regulation will apply to all AI systems placed on the market or put into service within the Union, regardless of their origin.

### Key Provisions

- **Risk Management**: AI systems will be classified based on risk levels: unacceptable, high, limited, and minimal.
  - *Unacceptable Risk*: Includes social scoring, real-time remote biometric identification, and mass surveillance.
  - *High Risk*: Includes credit scoring, employment, essential public services, and law enforcement.
  - *Limited Risk*: Includes AI systems used in recruitment, product safety, fraud detection, and more.
  - *Minimal Risk*: Includes AI systems that are transparent and explainable to users.

- **Obligations for Providers**: High-risk AI systems will require risk management measures, including data and algorithmic accuracy, robustness, cybersecurity, and human oversight.
- **Transparency**: Users must be informed about the use of AI, and providers must ensure transparency in their operations.
- **Human Oversight**: Critical functions such as law enforcement and essential public services must have human oversight.

### Implementation Arrangements

The AI Act will set up an European Board (EAB) to implement the AI Act and provide guidance. The EAB will work with national competent authorities to ensure compliance, monitor applications, and report on their effectiveness. The EAB will also cooperate with other EU agencies such as the European Data Protection Board.

### Governance

The governance structure includes a coordination group that brings together representatives from key stakeholder groups, including civil society organizations, social partners, and local and regional authorities. This structure aims to ensure that AI is developed in a way that respects fundamental rights and values.

## Gender Policy

The AI Act provides a gender assessment and GAP (Gender Assessment Plan) and therefore complies with the EU's gender equality policy.

### Key Provisions

- The general assessment provides the content of the reading environment that will be used to assess the impact on gender equality.
- The Commission on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women in 2001, the

# United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child in 1989 and the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities in 2006: 20% of PMD State Parties have ratified both conventions

The United Nations (UN) has adopted two key international human rights treaties that address the rights of children and persons with disabilities. The **Convention on the Rights of the Child** (CRC), adopted in 1989, is a comprehensive treaty that protects the rights of all children under the age of 18. The **Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities** (CRPD), adopted in 2006, is another landmark human rights instrument that promotes and protects the full enjoyment of human rights by persons with disabilities.

The CRC has been ratified by 196 States Parties, while the CRPD has been ratified by 183 States Parties. As of October 2021, only 40 States have ratified both conventions. This means that less than a quarter of all countries in the world have committed to upholding the rights of both children and persons with disabilities under international law.

The great achievement was completed based on a decade-long effort by civil society organizations, including disability rights groups, to ensure that the rights of persons with disabilities are protected under international human rights law. The CRC and CRPD are two of the most important human rights treaties in the world today. They provide a comprehensive framework for the protection and promotion of the rights of children and persons with disabilities.

The CRC was adopted by the UN General Assembly on November 20, 1989, and entered into force on September 2, 1990. It has 196 States Parties. The CRPD was adopted by the UN General Assembly on December 13, 2006, and entered into force on May 3, 2008. It has 183 States Parties.

The CRC and CRPD are both legally binding treaties that create obligations for States Parties to respect, protect, and fulfill the rights of children and persons with disabilities. They provide a comprehensive framework for the protection and promotion of these rights, including the right to education, health care, and social services. The conventions also include provisions on the participation of children and persons with disabilities in decision-making processes that affect their lives.

The CRC and CRPD are important tools for promoting and protecting the rights of children and persons with disabilities. # Green Climate Fund

## Summary

GCF has been implementing a number of climate change adaptation projects financed by external donors.

### a) Programme-specific risks (medium risk)

#### i. Duplication with other project activities

There have been a number of past and current projects that are similar to the proposed projects. The GCF has implemented several projects in the following countries: “Moçambique”, “SPV020”) which aim to address similar issues. The duplication is not expected to be significant as the proposed projects will be implemented in different locations. The duplication risk is considered medium.

#### ii. Implementation in remote locations

The AE identified the geographic remoteness of some project locations as a potential risk factor. This may increase the cost of travel and logistics. Some countries can be difficult to access due to their geographical location, which could complicate implementation and monitoring. The AE could serve as a financial and technical advisor for the projects in these remote areas. The risk is considered medium when working with the LA.

#### iii. Weak capacity of past project teams

Past projects have faced challenges related to weak local management capacities. Some past project interventions may have been unsuccessful due to weak local management capacities. The AE shared that the need for capacity building will be addressed differently in different States of FSM. The risk is considered medium as the proposed projects are expected to succeed with GCF grant resources and technical assistance from the AE. The risk is considered low if additional support is provided by FSM government personnel.

### b) Economic analysis and viability of the project

Green Climate Fund Board approved the proposal for the project. The project is expected to be implemented in a manner that will ensure its sustainability. The economic analysis was conducted by an independent consultant who has experience in climate change adaptation projects. The risk is considered low as the project is expected to be economically viable.

### c) Compliance with (national) laws

#### i. Compliance with the Public Corruption Act (PCA)

The Public Corruption Act (PCA) is a law that prohibits any individual or entity from engaging in corrupt activities. The PCA requires all individuals and entities involved in public projects to comply with the law. The GCF is required to ensure compliance with the PCA for all its projects. The risk is considered low as the project is expected to be compliant with the PCA.

#### ii. Compliance with the Foreign Corrupt Practices Act (FCPA)

The Foreign Corrupt Practices Act (FCPA) is a U.S. law that prohibits companies from bribing foreign officials to obtain or retain business. The GCF is required to comply with the FCPA for all its projects. The risk is considered low as the project is expected to be compliant with the FCPA.

# 4.4 Fiduciary

## a.
Patient Confidentiality (PC) will be the accredited entity and the executing entity for the project through a Defined SBA Program Oversight Unit (PSO), based within the PC.

## b.
SPC Client Groups and Environmental Sustainability Divisions will deliver tasks from their respective divisions. The PSO will oversee these activities, ensuring they are in line with the project requirements. The PSO will also ensure that all relevant information is shared with the SPC Client Groups and Environmental Sustainability Divisions to maintain integrity.

## c.
SPC will be responsible for the Financial Management and the financial auditing of GCP funds, executing its SPC plans and regulations. As the accredited entity, SPC's GCP within the project management will ensure that all financial activities are in line with the project requirements.

## d.
SPC will be responsible for the Training, Education, Reflection, and Learning (PEARL) process. SPC officials will be trained to work with the SPC Client Groups and Environmental Sustainability Divisions on the PEARL process. Counting the Existing of "Formalism", "Professionalism" and "Clients and Sub-contractors" will be well documented in the project. The existing processes and procedures will be followed in the implementation of the projects.

# 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

## a.
As an administrative programme, the intervention expects to benefit about 54,000 direct and 200,000 indirect beneficiaries, which means a coverage of 8 per cent and 36 per cent respectively. The project will be implemented in two phases: the first phase will focus on the implementation of the project activities, while the second phase will focus on the monitoring and evaluation of the project.

## b.
The revised logical framework designates most of the components and segments that are expected to be achieved at different stages of the project. The revised logical framework will be used as a guide for the project implementation and monitoring process.

## c.
The implementation schedule is annexed S1 and aligned with the programme Calendar under activity 11 in the first sheet, where the monitoring and evaluation plan is annexed 11 in the second sheet. This will ensure that all activities are implemented according to the revised logical framework.

## Proposed title
Climate change adaptation solutions for local authorities in the Federated States of Micronesia

Federated States of Micronesia

## Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential
#### Scale: High

The Federated States of Micronesia (FSM) is an archipelago located in the North Pacific Ocean, comprising over 600 islands spread across 2.5 million square kilometers of ocean. The four states are Pohnpei, Chuuk, Kosrae and Yap. The climate is tropical with a mean annual temperature of around 27°C and high humidity. The FSM has a population of approximately 113,000 people.

The climate of the FSM exhibits strong seasonality and inter-annual variability. While El Niño events are associated with reduced rainfall and increased air temperatures, La Niña events are associated with increased rainfall and cooler temperatures. The climate change impacts on the FSM are expected to be significant. Sea level rise is already affecting coastal communities, particularly in low-lying atolls. Increased frequency of extreme weather events such as tropical cyclones, droughts and floods will exacerbate existing vulnerabilities.

# Green Climate Fund

## Resilience

The majority of the proposed fund (€e USD 17 billion out of a total USD 15.71 billion) is reserved for the proposed risk and liquidity facility. The project includes a budget of USD 30 million to cover administrative costs and overheads.

If per event the ESM contribution (USD 200 million) will benefit directly, indirect benefits are expected to be significant as well. The project will contribute to the resilience of the financial sector by reducing the need for public funds to bail out banks in times of stress. This will reduce the moral hazard and encourage banks to improve their risk management practices. The project will also contribute to the creation of a market for liquidity insurance, which is expected to be an important component of the ESM's crisis response toolkit.

# Green Climate Fund

## Environmental co-benefits

### Ecosystem co-benefits

- Reducing the risk and damage from disasters through improved water management can enhance ecosystem services.
- Improved water management can reduce the risk of floods and landslides, and by sequestering carbon in the mangroves.

### Social co-benefits

- Benefits that accrue from improved weather events are directly beneficial to communities in all countries. The activities may be in place to address well-documented risks. The benefits will include reduced mortality and morbidity, reduced health care costs, and increased agricultural productivity.
- Improved water management can also reduce the risk of disasters, offering opportunities to be more resilient to health care facilities and infrastructure.
- Improved water management can also improve social outcomes in terms of enhanced employment and reduction of poverty.
- Improved water management can also enhance livelihoods through improved access to water for agriculture and other uses. This will increase agricultural productivity and incomes, which will have a positive impact on the local economy.

### Gender-related co-benefits

1. In a society where community-based natural resource management (NRM) efforts are predominantly considered, it is often assumed that women's participation in decision-making processes is limited to household levels.
2. Women's participation in water management can be enhanced by providing them with the necessary skills and knowledge to participate effectively in decision-making processes at all levels of governance.
3. Women's participation in water management can also enhance their ability to access and control resources, which will have a positive impact on their livelihoods.

### Needs of the recipient

- Scale: High

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## c)

The project will be implemented over seven years at an estimated cost (USD) 147.1 million. The project proposes a total of USD 15.9 million in the form of grants. Considering the grant component, the project will be financed by the Government of India through the Department of Economic Affairs (DEA), Ministry of Finance. The co-financing amount is about USD 132 million, most of which is provided by the FSM.

## d)

As mentioned in the earlier sections, there is a dearth of financing for climate change mitigation projects in developing countries. This project will provide an opportunity to leverage additional finance from the private sector and other sources. The project will also be key in leveraging adaptive financing mechanisms that are available for boosting resilience to FSM. The capacity of financing institutions to critically important.

## e)

The project proposes supplementary loans that can enhance the value for money as the government will be able to borrow at a lower cost than commercial banks. The project will be implemented by the Department of Economic Affairs (DEA), Ministry of Finance, Government of India.

## f)

The long-term vision between India, a large sub-national entity, and high-income countries, leverages and brings together the best practices from both sides. This is an opportunity to demonstrate that developing countries can also be leaders in addressing climate change mitigation. The project will be implemented by the Department of Economic Affairs (DEA), Ministry of Finance, Government of India.

# I.

The entire and small scale adaptive response are yet to be defined in an EBA financing arrangement. However, the broad categories of responses against most known hazards are well understood. The EBA will continue to develop its own guidelines for these responses as part of the EBA grant-based projects. Since the responses will be evaluated against a set of criteria, as in the case of other EBA grants, the project will be able to provide lessons learned towards improving industry best practices. Moreover, the nature of small grants under the EBA financing arrangement will allow for the development of innovative solutions that enhance the adoption of industry best practices. The project will also provide a platform to monitor and evaluate the effectiveness and efficiency of the project are high.

# II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The proposed EBA project is a good example of this kind of project, and the independent TAP recommends the Board to approve the project.

# Overview

The Gender Analysis and Action Plan (GAAP) provides the overall context and framework for managing gender via the Green Climate Fund (GCF) project climate change mitigation activities. This document is a companion to the GCF Gender Policy and the GCF Gender Mainstreaming Guide and includes the following actions:

- Overview of the region and people from which the women's groups are general in the Pacific Island Countries
- Analysis of gender-based violence, health, education, and income data from the 2017 Census and other sources
- Description of issues and employment in the agricultural sector by gender for the PIM
- Overview of existing gender issues in the SAM
- Overview of existing gender issues in the GCF Gender Policy
- Assessment of gender-based violence and gender assessment for gender equity
- Development of a gender indicator framework and gender assessment protocols and regional assessment
- Development of a gender indicator framework for FSM and other Pacific Island Countries
- Assessment of gender considerations for priority sectors areas and activities
- Description of adult implementation issues and responsibilities with regards to gender
- Overall Gender Action Plan for the sub-grant activities
- Assessment for gender equity in the sub-grant

# Methodology

This GAAP was completed through a desk review of FSM's staff and people framework and closely aligns with the GCF Gender Policy. The methodology used for this analysis is consistent with the approach outlined in the GCF Gender Policy and includes the following steps:

1. Princess Women's Council
2. Pohnpei Women's Council
3. Chuuk Women's Council
4. Kosrae Women's Council

The assessment for the FSM Women's Council was completed in person; however, due to the ongoing COVID-19 pandemic, the remaining consultations were conducted via virtual meetings by the national women's councils.

The consultation focused on how gender vulnerabilities matched with the three thematic areas covered by the proposed EMA programs (including risk reduction and coastal protection, food security and water resources, and climate change adaptation). Through these consultations, local safety groups and existing women's human rights and gender equity issues were identified. The consultations also included a review of the summary of women's group recommendations collected from the national women's councils.

# Gender Analysis and Assessment

## Legal and Policy Framework

International and Regional treaties and agreements that FSM is party to and supports:

- **Paris Agreement (Paris Protocol)**: Adopted in 2015 by the Conference of the Parties to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), it aims to limit global warming to below 2 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels.
- **Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities**: Ratified by the FSM Government in November 2016, this convention promotes and protects the human rights of people with disabilities.
- **Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)**: Adopted by the UN General Assembly in 1979, it aims to eliminate discrimination against women.
- **Convention on the Rights of the Child**: Ratified by the FSM Government in November 2003, this convention sets out the rights of children and the responsibilities of governments to protect these rights.
- **Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD)**: Adopted at the Earth Summit in Rio de Janeiro in 1992, it aims to promote sustainable development by encouraging countries to conserve biological diversity.
- **United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC)**: Signed in 1994 and ratified in 1995, this convention aims to stabilize greenhouse gas concentrations in the atmosphere at a level that would prevent dangerous anthropogenic interference with the climate system.
- **Agenda 2030 for Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)**: Adopted by all United Nations Member States in 2015, it provides a shared blueprint for peace and prosperity for people and the planet.
- **Refugee Convention**: Adopted in 1951 and entered into force in 1954, this convention defines who is a refugee and sets out the rights of refugees and the legal obligations of states to protect them.

Examples of Recommendations include:

# Pacific Women Shaping Pacific Development

- Ensure that services, especially rural women, children, persons with disabilities and marginalised and vulnerable groups, have access to gender responsive and inclusive health and social protection mechanisms.
- Strengthen the capacity of national and regional institutions to implement the Pacific Regional Ending Violence against Women (EVAW) Framework for National Action Plans on Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment.

## Strategic Objectives

### Objective 1: Enhanced Capacity of National and Regional Institutions
- Strengthen the capacity of national and regional institutions to implement the Pacific Regional Ending Violence against Women (EVAW) Framework.
- Support the development of national action plans for gender equality and women’s empowerment.

### Objective 2: Improved Access to Services
- Ensure that services, especially rural women, children, persons with disabilities and marginalised and vulnerable groups, have access to gender responsive and inclusive health and social protection mechanisms.
- Strengthen the capacity of national and regional institutions to implement the Pacific Regional Ending Violence against Women (EVAW) Framework.

# FSM Strategic Development Plan (2004-2023)

The FSM Strategic Development Plan (2004-2023) outlines seven specific strategic goals for achieving sustained and equitable economic growth in the Federated States of Micronesia (FSM).

## Strategy Goals

### Strategy Goal 1: Enhance women's participation in economic activities, lead political and social development of women and children through education.

- Ensure equal access to quality education.
- Promote gender equity in all sectors.
- Provide health care services for women and children.

### Strategy Goal 2: Enhance women's contribution to and participation in economic and social development.

- Encourage women's involvement in decision-making processes.
- Promote women's leadership roles.

### Strategy Goal 3: Strengthen gender issues in decision making, policies and strategies.

- Integrate gender equality into national planning.
- Ensure gender-responsive budgeting.

### Strategy Goal 4: Enhance women's contribution to and participation in economic and social development.

- Encourage women's involvement in decision-making processes.
- Promote women's leadership roles.

### Strategy Goal 5: Strengthen the institutional capacity of the women's programs in FSM.

- Provide resources for women's programs.
- Ensure gender-responsive budgeting.

### Strategy Goal 6: Enhance youth development through social, economic and political participation.

- Provide education and training opportunities.
- Promote youth leadership roles.

### Strategy Goal 7: Strengthen youth development through social, economic and political participation.

- Provide education and training opportunities.
- Promote youth leadership roles.

## National Gender Policy

Adopted in 2018, the strategy aims to achieve gender equality by addressing issues such as:

- Goal 1: Equal access to quality education for women and children.
- Goal 2: Elimination of gender-based violence.
- Goal 3: Equal participation in decision-making processes.
- Goal 4: Equal access to economic resources.
- Goal 5: Equal access to health care services.

## The FSM Policy Recognizes

The policy recognizes the importance of gender equality and the need for:

- Gender equity in all sectors.
- Equal opportunities for women and men.
- Equal participation in decision-making processes.

### The FSM National Strategic Plan

The plan highlights specific goals including promoting gender equity, ensuring equal access to education, health care, and economic resources. It also emphasizes the importance of addressing issues such as:

- Gender-based violence.
- Women's leadership roles.
- Youth development through social, economic, and political participation.

### Additional Policy Framework

Laws and programs supporting include:

- The FSM Constitution Article 17, section 4 ensures equal protection, which guarantees equality before the law, non-discrimination, and equal rights for all citizens regardless of gender, origin, language, and social status.
- Family Protection Act 2014 (Public Law 18-56) passed by Congress.
- In 2019, the FSM Government obtained the Age of Consent from ages 13 to 18 (FSM CL No. 6-06).

## The FID Native Lands Act of 2019

Highlighting the need to govern governance and development, the FID Native Lands Act of 2019 is a landmark legislation that aims to:

- Budget for the national and state programs over one operational cycle.
- Allocate safety, travel, and other expenses from the General Fund.
- Provide funding for the development of the FID Native Lands Act of 2019.
- Ensure the allocation of funds for the development of the FID Native Lands Act of 2019.

This issue is an embodiment in complete risk analysis and community buy-in from local leaders and members of the community.

- GDO has few resources and no process to facilitate the dissemination of gender and women’s issues within the organization.

- GDO has expertise and experience in gender and human rights analyses and investigation, including the use of gender-based analysis plus (GBA+). This expertise can be used for:
  - Gender-based analysis
  - Advocacy management
  - Program development

Furthermore it should be noted that in addition to the GDO, FSM National Government funds some gender-related activities work in Choiseul and Chuuk. In particular, CEDAW’s resulting in state women’s offices in Chuuk and Pohnpei is largely a result of these programs. Information and advocacy through these offices has been instrumental in providing information and in particular through the sub-committee on gender issues where community representatives are involved.

Since the addition to the FSM Gender Desk, Stockdale, the Department of Health and Social Affairs and GDO have made significant progress including:

- Increasing awareness and understanding of gender issues
- Improving and increasing capacity to incorporate gender equality considerations in cultural government processes (for example the SDGs and the National Development Plan)

# Existing Gender Inequality - Statistical Overview

The overall profile for FML highlights that the natural level about one-fifth of the population lives in female-headed households. This is significantly higher than the average across all states except FSM. Further, at the national level, all of the Marshall Islands and 10 out of 16 states exceed this rate.

## Table: Male vs Female-Headed Households - Summary 2013/2014 HHES

| Household Type | Percent of Households |
|----------------|-----------------------|
| Male Headed    | 85.7%                 |
| Female Headed  | 14.3%                 |

### Male-Headed Households

| State/Territory | Percent of Households |
|-----------------|-----------------------|
| Chuuk           | 90.6%                 |
| Kosrae          | 87.5%                 |
| Pohnpei         | 84.3%                 |
| Yap             | 82.1%                 |

### Female-Headed Households

| State/Territory | Percent of Households |
|-----------------|-----------------------|
| Chuuk           | 9.4%                  |
| Kosrae          | 12.5%                 |
| Pohnpei         | 15.7%                 |
| Yap             | 17.9%                 |

In 2013/2014, male-headed households across all states had higher average annual income than female-headed households. However, significant variation exists among the states and islands. For example, Chuuk has the highest average annual household income at $58,600, while Yap has the lowest at $29,300. The overall gender difference in average annual household income is $17,400 (65.2% vs 47.8%).

## Table: Average Annual Household Income by Gender

| State/Territory | Male Headed Income | Female Headed Income |
|-----------------|--------------------|---------------------|
| Chuuk           | $58,600            | $31,200             |
| Kosrae          | $47,900            | $28,300             |
| Pohnpei         | $41,800            | $24,400             |
| Yap             | $29,300            | $16,500             |

Source of income across male and female-headed households is consistent for the most part, with the major difference being that male-headed households are on average 17.8% more based on cash income.

# and formal headed households earned 9% more than male headed households.

What the experiences of these countries suggest is that gender equality in education is a key factor in achieving higher levels of economic development. In particular, it appears to be associated with higher levels of female labor force participation for 24.8% and 35.0% of monthly expenditures, respectively. Even if it is a substantial increase in the number of women who are employed full-time.

In the workforce, gender disparities exist. Across the States, 67.7% of Chads to 9.3% (Phupped) the main difference between men and women in the labor force is that more women work part-time jobs. For example, only 15.8% of women are employed full-time compared to 40.2% of men. In addition, women are more likely to be engaged in home-based work and 67.9% engaged in non-based work 35.0% of households. In addition, the number of women who are employed in the workforce is higher than that of men. The other major difference is that 14.8% of women are employed full-time compared to 24.8% of men. These differences highlight that economic opportunities for women are limited by their lack of access to education and training, as well as their lack of access to the labor market.

The gender gap in employment is also found in the FIM Survey. Recently, women have been more likely to be employed full-time than men. In 1975, only 34.8% of women were employed compared to 60.2% of men. In addition, women are more likely to be engaged in home-based work and 67.9% engaged in non-based work 35.0% of households. In addition, the number of women who are employed in the workforce is higher than that of men. The other major difference is that 14.8% of women are employed full-time compared to 24.8% of men. These differences highlight that economic opportunities for women are limited by their lack of access to education and training, as well as their lack of access to the labor market.

For example, women in FIM have significantly lower educational attainment than men. In the same survey, only 10.2% of women had completed high school compared to 35.0% of men. However, this difference is more pronounced in Yala and Kowei where 40.2% and 67.9% of women had completed high school compared to 80.2% and 90.2% of men, respectively. Overall, about 40.2% of women had completed high school compared to 54.1% of men. This difference is also reflected in the proportion of women who are employed full-time compared to 24.8% of men.

Further, the 2017 Family Atlas for FIM further documents issues for gender inequality in FIM is highlighted by the fact that only about 35.0% of women have completed high school compared to 67.9% of men. In addition, women are more likely to be engaged in home-based work and 67.9% engaged in non-based work 35.0% of households. In addition, the number of women who are employed in the workforce is higher than that of men. The other major difference is that 14.8% of women are employed full-time compared to 24.8% of men. These differences highlight that economic opportunities for women are limited by their lack of access to education and training, as well as their lack of access to the labor market.

# Pacific Communities Health District

## 2018 Agricultural Census

In the 2018 Agricultural Census, near to the people aged 15 and over households with land used for farming were identified. The census found that 67% of farmers were male and 33% female. Additionally, 94% of males reported having graduated from high school, and 11% of females reported the same.

The FSM Education Indicators Report for 2018/2019 highlights that in 2018, just enrollees in FSM schools is 67% in elementary level, whereas it is only 65% in ECE and 48% in secondary level. While boys and girls are equally represented at the elementary level, there is a significant gender gap at higher levels of education. Boys outnumber girls by nearly two to one in high school and by more than three to one in college.

Possible reasons for this variation include cultural expectations that limit the ability of girls to pursue education beyond secondary school. However, other factors such as family income, parental education, and school environment may also play a role. In addition, the gender gap is most pronounced among students from lower-income families. These indicators in designing projects, communications, and other strategies for the program. Further, the FSM Education Indicators Report 2018/2019 highlights that girls are more likely to drop out of school due to early marriage, pregnancy, and child rearing responsibilities.

The ECE program should also provide a safe learning environment for all students.

# Women in Pacific Leadership Positions in FSM States

There are few women serving as legislators in the Federated States of Micronesia (FSM) and in Chuuk. There were no women serving in the public service until recently when a woman was appointed to the position of Secretary of Health and Social Affairs. Women have achieved seniority and are able to influence government policies and programs. Women's voices show that at the local level, women hold leadership positions in their communities and are involved in decision-making processes related to family, community, and religious activities.

## Figure 1: Women in Pacific Leadership Positions in FSM States

There are few women serving as legislators in the Federated States of Micronesia (FSM) and in Chuuk. There were no women serving in the public service until recently when a woman was appointed to the position of Secretary of Health and Social Affairs. Women have achieved seniority and are able to influence government policies and programs. Women's voices show that at the local level, women hold leadership positions in their communities and are involved in decision-making processes related to family, community, and religious activities.

### Agricultural Income and Participation

The 2018 FSM Agricultural Census provides a more detailed view of who uses and cultivates the agricultural sector (ing: raising, harvesting, farming, and hunting) which helps provide additional income for families. The census also provides information on the use of labor and household characteristics.

Just over 90% households reported at the agriculture sector had access to land for cultivation. About half of these households had access to land for agricultural activities (57%). About 38% of households had access to land for agricultural activities but did not cultivate it. The majority of households that cultivated land were male-headed.

# Pacific Institute for Research and Evaluation

## Violence Against Women

### October 2015 FSM conducted a National Family Health and Safety Study which highlighted the major issues of violence against women in FSM including the following significant findings:

- Alcohol use is three times more prevalent among women than men (38.9%) who experienced physical assault by an intimate partner.
- 46% of women who experienced sexual assault were under the influence of alcohol at the time of the incident, compared to 17% of men.
- 25% of women and 10% of men reported experiencing emotional abuse by a partner in the past year.
- 39.8% of women and 16.4% of men experienced controlling behavior from their partners.

Some key recommendations for FGM included that national and state level actions are implemented to:

- Promote a multi-sectoral coordination between the health system and other public agencies (e.g., education, social services) to address FGM.
- Provide training and support to health care providers to identify and appropriately refer affected women and children. Include those sharing duties by a community health worker.
- Establish workshops for parents about parent-child relationships, gender roles, and gender equality.
- Promote the passing of the Family Protection Act in Papua, Chukot, and Yap.
- Give funding priority to existing government programs aimed at addressing violence against women and children, including domestic violence.

# Comparison to the Region

Compared to other regions in the Pacific, FSM is lagging on major gender indicators (as collected by the SPC). The FSM has a high maternal mortality ratio, low female participation in the labor force, and employment of women employed in non-agricultural sectors.

## Gender Mainstreaming Considerations for Sub-grant Activities

There are three principal sectors for the sub-grants: climate responsiveness; food security; water resource management. Each sub-grant will be required to address at least one of these principal sectors. An overarching goal is to provide an enabling environment for women’s empowerment and gender equality.

# Root Causes

- Socially-constructed and cultural norms create a lack of consciousness about women's and children's rights and needs to:
  - Increase fear regarding health services and other support systems.
  - Decrease access to information and resources for prevention and treatment.
  - Increase stigma against women who become pregnant during times of conflict.

# Causes

- Conflicts in the home and community create a lack of awareness about reproductive health, increase exposure to violence, and reduce access to services. This includes:
  - Increased risk of malnutrition and sexual abuse and other reproductive health risks.
  - Limited access to family planning and reproductive health services.
  - Limited availability of safe water, creating unsafe conditions for children.
  - Limited access to safe water, creating unsafe conditions for children.

# Problem Statement

Women are vulnerable to the impacts of climate change particularly related to increased disaster risk and recovery capabilities, increased food insecurity, and decreased water security. This is exacerbated by gender inequality and social exclusion.

# Immediate Effects

- Increased burden of care and work for women who leave the household to find food.
  - Increased burden of care and work for women who leave the household to find food.
  - Increased burden of care and work for women who leave the household to find food.

# Effects

- Increased food and water insecurity for vulnerable households with limited means for recovery.
  - Increased food and water insecurity for vulnerable households with limited means for recovery.

# Coastal Descriptions

## Table Key Gender Considerations for DRIF Food Security and Water Security with Women

### Coastal Descriptions

- In many coastal communities, women are involved in the collection of shellfish, seaweed, and other marine resources.
- Women also play a significant role in post-harvest processing such as drying, salting, and preserving fish for consumption or sale.
- Men, on the other hand, are more likely to be involved in fishing activities that require boats and other equipment.
- Women's roles in coastal communities often go unrecognized and undervalued, yet they are critical to the sustainability of these communities.

### Food Security

- Gender roles in food security need to be recognized. Women play a significant role in ensuring household food security through their involvement in agriculture, fishing, and other food-related activities.
- Women's access to resources such as land, credit, and technology is often limited, which can hinder their ability to fully participate in food production and marketing.
- Gender inequalities in the distribution of labor and resources within households can also affect women's food security.

### Water Security

- Women are responsible for collecting water in many coastal communities. This task can be time-consuming and physically demanding, especially when water sources are distant or unreliable.
- Women's access to safe and reliable water sources is crucial for their health, well-being, and productivity.
- Gender inequalities in water management and decision-making processes can limit women's ability to participate in water-related activities and benefit from water security initiatives.

### Key Gender Considerations

- Women may lack access to resources such as credit, land, and technology that are essential for food production and marketing.
- Women's roles in food security need to be recognized and valued. They play a critical role in ensuring household food security through their involvement in agriculture, fishing, and other food-related activities.
- Gender inequalities in the distribution of labor and resources within households can affect women's food security.
- Women are responsible for collecting water in many coastal communities. This task can be time-consuming and physically demanding, especially when water sources are distant or unreliable.
- Women's access to safe and reliable water sources is crucial for their health, well-being, and productivity.
- Gender inequalities in water management and decision-making processes can limit women's ability to participate in water-related activities and benefit from water security initiatives.

### References

- Gender and Climate Change: A Guidebook for Integrating Gender into Climate Change Adaptation (2014). United Nations Entity for Gender Equality and Empowerment of Women (UN Women).
- Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations. Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment in Agriculture: A Conceptual Framework for Country Level Analysis.

# Women's Household Responsibilities

If "women's household responsibilities" are implicitly included in the definition of "household," it is difficult to make a strong claim that women are not represented in the [FISH] framework. However, this is an issue worth exploring further. What is evident is that women's roles and responsibilities are often overlooked when designing programs for water, sanitation, and hygiene (WASH) services. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes related to WASH services because of their household responsibilities. This exclusion can lead to a lack of understanding about the needs and preferences of women users.

## Water, Sanitation, and Hygiene

Women play an important role in managing water resources and ensuring that their families have access to safe drinking water. Women are also responsible for collecting firewood and other fuels needed for cooking and heating. In many cases, women are the primary caregivers for children and the elderly, which can make it difficult for them to participate in community activities or take on leadership roles.

### Recommendations

Given the above context for gender, disaster risk and recovery, food security water security, and climate change in FISH, the following recommendations are provided to help ensure effective gender responsive programming:

1. **Gender Expertise for Screening and Review of Sub-Grants:** Given the decentralized nature of sub-grants, it is important that gender expertise be included as part of the overall program. This includes ensuring that gender experts are involved in the screening and review process. Gender experts should also be charged with monitoring and evaluating (M&E) as well as resource allocation to ensure that sub-grant projects are responsive to the needs of women.

2. **Gender Mainstreaming:** Women play an important role in managing and using water resources, and their participation is essential for effective WASH programming. Women's roles and responsibilities should be taken into account when designing and implementing programs. This includes ensuring that women have access to safe drinking water and sanitation facilities, as well as opportunities to participate in decision-making processes related to water management.

3. **Gender-Sensitive Programming:** Gender-sensitive programming is essential for ensuring that women's needs are met. This includes providing adequate resources for women to participate in WASH programs, as well as ensuring that programs are designed to meet the specific needs of women and girls.

4. **Capacity Building:** Capacity building is an important aspect of gender-responsive programming. This includes providing training and support to women and girls to help them develop the skills they need to participate effectively in WASH programs.

5. **Monitoring and Evaluation:** Monitoring and evaluation are essential for ensuring that gender-responsive programming is effective. This includes collecting data on the impact of programs on women and girls, as well as using this data to inform program design and implementation.


---
**Page 130**
---

# Sub-Grant Design

1. **Incorporation of Gender into Sub-Grant Design:** In addition to the Grants Program’s overall gender integration efforts, sub-grantees will be encouraged to incorporate gender considerations in their project design and implementation. This includes ensuring that women are included as the daily technical experts and recommenders, gender desegregated data is collected, and gender-responsive approaches are used to address gender gaps in programming and new initiatives.

2. **Integration of Gender-Responsive Activities into Sub-Grant Design:** The ECO Family will integrate gender-responsive activities into sub-grant design. This includes ensuring that all sub-grantees have the capacity to implement gender-responsive activities, such as training for women’s groups, implementing monitoring, gender mainstreaming, and developing gender-responsive approaches.

3. **Gender-Responsive Monitoring and Evaluation:** Gender indicators will be included in the monitoring framework of the sub-grant program. This includes tracking progress towards gender equality objectives related to knowledge and skills development, leadership, and empowerment with IAA, such as developing sub-grants. Evaluating the impact of these activities on women’s empowerment and gender equality is critical for ensuring that sub-grant programs support the sustainability of gender-equitable results.

4. **Gender-Responsive Budgeting:** Gender-responsive budgeting will be integrated into the sub-grant design process. This includes ensuring that all sub-grantees have the capacity to implement gender-responsive activities, such as training for women’s groups, implementing monitoring, gender mainstreaming, and developing gender-responsive approaches.

5. **Inclusion of Women's Organizations in Sub-Grant Design and Implementation:** There is a need to ensure that women’s organizations are included in sub-grant design and implementation. This includes ensuring that women’s organizations have the capacity to implement gender-responsive activities, such as training for women’s groups, implementing monitoring, gender mainstreaming, and developing gender-responsive approaches.

6. **Empowerment of Women:** Empowerment is achieved by the ability to be heard for women. It is essential to provide opportunities for women to participate in decision-making processes at all levels. Sub-grant will assist in ensuring that women have an equal opportunity to influence the results through direct engagement and leadership.

7. **Target Hard-to-Reach Women:** Women are often hard-to-reach, including those living in remote areas, displaced persons, and those with disabilities. This includes ensuring that sub-grantees have the capacity to implement gender-responsive activities, such as training for women’s groups, implementing monitoring, gender mainstreaming, and developing gender-responsive approaches.

8. **Partnerships with Women:** Partnerships with women are essential in ensuring that women have an equal opportunity to participate in decision-making processes at all levels. This includes ensuring that sub-grantees have the capacity to implement gender-responsive activities, such as training for women’s groups, implementing monitoring, gender mainstreaming, and developing gender-responsive approaches.

# Gender Action Plan

## Implementation Roles and Responsibilities for Gender Mainstreaming

The various entities within the EDA Facility are responsible for achieving gender mainstreaming and the effective execution of the gender action plan. In each case, they have unique and complementary roles and responsibilities.

- **EDA Coordination Unit - SPC**: Through the setting up of an ECI is responsible for overall compliance with the GSP goals, policy and the monitoring/implementing of GSP and facilitates the implementation of the Gender Action Plan (GAP). It will ensure that all entities within the EDA Facility are gender mainstreamed in their respective planning and review processes, particularly for gender mainstreaming used in the sub-grants. The SPC is also responsible for ensuring that the sub-grantees and the implementing agents adhere to the principles of gender equality and non-discrimination. It will ensure that all sub-grantees are accountable against the gender indicators and provide technical support to the sub-grantees in their implementation.

- **FMA Department of Finance and Administration (DFA)**: The NGA works alongside SPC to ensure compliance with GSP goals, policy and monitoring/implementation. Both these units will pursue close collaboration to ensure that all EDA Facility entities are gender mainstreamed in their respective planning and review processes. It will also work closely with the sub-grantees to ensure that they adhere to the principles of gender equality and non-discrimination. Both these units will work closely with the sub-grantees to ensure that they adhere to the principles of gender equality and non-discrimination.

- **NGA**: The NGA is responsible for carrying out all activities related to promoting, ensuring that SPC procurement policy is fully aligned properly with education to gender equality and non-discrimination. It will also work closely with the sub-grantees to ensure that they adhere to the principles of gender equality and non-discrimination.

- **SPC Staff**: LAO will coordinate with the ECI and work closely with the implementing agents to develop and implement gender equality policies, guidelines and procedures. This will necessarily include developing and implementing a gender equality policy for the SPC staff and ensuring that all sub-grantees adhere to this policy. Additionally, it may also be key focal points for the collection of gender disaggregated data as a basis for monitoring and evaluation.

- **Facilitating Gender**: It is work to provide capacity for aid grant development and design, including the integration of gender equality principles into all aspects of the sub-grants. This includes ensuring that specific requirements by gender-responsive design in aid grants. Additionally, it has to be the key focal points for the collection of gender disaggregated data as a basis for monitoring and evaluation.

# The Facilitating Agents will be providing technical assistance for the design and implementation of the project.

The Facilitating Agents will be providing technical assistance for the design and implementation of the project. The Facilitating Agents, as part of their quarterly site visits to sub-grantee project sites, will have the opportunity to observe and document lessons learned from the sub-grantees' experiences. These lessons learned benefits, including sharing to GIRV. Grantsees will then be passed on to the ECU.

- Local governments have undertaken a gender audit to identify areas for improvement.

### Ongoing

- 25% of communities are engaged in the planning process.
- 75% of communities are engaged in the planning process.
